Ephemeral resources always produce ephemeral values.\n - **Ephemeral values**: Input variables and outputs can now be defined as ephemeral. Ephemeral values may only be used in certain contexts in Terraform configuration, and are not persisted to the plan or state files.\n    - **`ephemeralasnull` function**: a function takes a value of any type and returns a similar value of the same type with any ephemeral values replaced with non-ephemeral null values and all non-ephemeral values preserved.\n\nBUG FIXES:\n\n- The `secret_suffix` in the `kubernetes` backend now includes validation to prevent errors when the `secret_suffix` ends with a number ([#35666](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/pull/35666)).\n- The error message for an invalid default value for an input variable now indicates when the problem is with a nested value in a complex data type. ([#35465](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35465))\n- Sensitive marks could be incorrectly transferred to nested resource values, causing erroneous changes during a plan ([#35501](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35501))\n- Allow unknown `error_message` values to pass the core validate step, so variable validation can be completed later during plan\n  ([#35537](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35537))\n- Unencoded slashes within GitHub module source refs were being truncated and incorrectly used as subdirectories in the request path ([#35552](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35552))\n- Terraform refresh-only plans with output only changes are now applyable. ([#35812](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35812))\n- Postconditions referencing `self` with many instances could encounter an error during evaluation ([#35895](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35895))\n- The `plantimestamp()` function would return an invalid date during validation ([#35902](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35902))\n- Updates to resources which were forced to use create_before_destroy could lose that flag in the state temporarily and cause cycles if immediately removed from the configuration ([#35966](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35966))\n- backend/cloud: Prefer KV tags, even when tags are defined as set ([#35937](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/pull/35937))\n- Simplify config generation (`plan -generate-config-out`) for string attributes that contain primitive types (e.g. numbers or booleans) ([#35984](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/pull/35984))\n- config: `issensitive` could incorrectly assert that an unknown value was not sensitive during plan, but later became sensitive during apply, causing failures where changes did not match the planned result ([#36012](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/36012))\n- config: The evaluation of conditional expressions and for expression in HCL could lose marks with certain combinations of unknown values ([#36017](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/36017))\n\nENHANCEMENTS:\n\n- The `element` function now accepts negative indices ([#35501](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35501))\n- Import block validation has been improved to provide more useful errors and catch more invalid cases during `terraform validate` ([#35543](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35543))\n- Performance enhancements for resource evaluation, especially when large numbers of resource instances are involved ([#35558](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35558))\n- The `plan`, `apply`, and `refresh` commands now produce a deprecated warning when using the `-state` flag. Instead use the `path` attribute within the `local` backend to modify the state file. ([#35660](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35660))\n- backend/cos: Add new auth for Tencent Cloud backend ([#35888](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/pull/35888))\n\nUPGRADE NOTES:\n\n- backend/s3: Removes deprecated attributes for assuming IAM role. Must use the `assume_role` block ([#35721](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35721))\n- backend/s3: The s3 backend now supports S3 native state locking. When used with DynamoDB-based locking, locks will be acquired from both sources. In a future minor release of Terraform the DynamoDB locking mechanism and associated arguments will be deprecated. ([#35661](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35661))\n- `moved`: Moved blocks now respect reserved keywords when parsing resource addresses. Configurations that reference resources with type names that match top level blocks and keywords from `moved` blocks will need to prepend the `resource.` identifier to these references. ([#35850](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35850))\n- config: In order to ensure consistency in results from HCL conditional expressions, marks must be combined from all values within the expression to avoid losing mark information. This typically improves accuracy when validating configuration, but users may see sensitive results where they were lost previously.\n\n## Previous Releases\n\nFor information on prior major and minor releases, refer to their changelogs:\n\n- [v1.9](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v1.9/CHANGELOG.md)\n- [v1.8](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v1.8/CHANGELOG.md)\n- [v1.7](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v1.7/CHANGELOG.md)\n- [v1.6](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v1.6/CHANGELOG.md)\n- [v1.5](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v1.5/CHANGELOG.md)\n- [v1.4](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v1.4/CHANGELOG.md)\n- [v1.3](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v1.3/CHANGELOG.md)\n- [v1.2](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v1.2/CHANGELOG.md)\n- [v1.1](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v1.1/CHANGELOG.md)\n- [v1.0](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v1.0/CHANGELOG.md)\n- [v0.15](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v0.15/CHANGELOG.md)\n- [v0.14](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v0.14/CHANGELOG.md)\n- [v0.13](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v0.13/CHANGELOG.md)\n- [v0.12](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v0.12/CHANGELOG.md)\n- [v0.11 and earlier](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/blob/v0.11/CHANGELOG.md)","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"ceac232f-983d-4180-b05d-2dff83d40a7a","uuid":"b640f03c-f233-4580-b9f2-256591ebafb6","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"## 1.10.0 (November 27, 2024)\n\nNEW FEATURES:\n - **Ephemeral resources**: Ephemeral resources are read anew during each phase of Terraform evaluation, and cannot be persisted to state storage. The `moved` block programmatically changes the location of a resource.\n---\n\n# Moved block configuration reference\n\nThis topic provides reference information for the `moved` block. \n\n## Introduction\n\nThe `moved` block programmatically changes the address of a resource. Refer to [Refactoring](/terraform/language/modules/develop/refactoring) for details about how to use the `moved` block in your Terraform configurations.\n\n## Configuration model\n\nThe following list outlines field hierarchy, language-specific data types, and requirements in the `moved` block. \n\n- [`moved`](#moved): map\n  - [`from`](#moved): reference\n  - [`to`](#moved): reference\n\n## Complete configuration\n\nWhen every field is defined, a `moved` block has the following form:\n\n```hcl\nmoved {\n    from = <old address for the resource>\n    to = <new address for the resource>\n}\n```\n\n## Specification\n\nThis section provides details about the fields you can configure in the `moved` block.\n\n### `moved`\n\nMap that specifies addresses for the resource. The following table describes the fields you can set in the `moved` block.\n\n| Field | Description | Type | Required |\n| ---   | ---         | ---  | ---      |\n| `from` | Specifies a resource's previous address. The syntax allows Terraform to select modules, resources, and resources inside child modules. | string | required |\n| `to` | Specifies the new address to relocate the resource to. The syntax allows Terraform to select modules, resources, and resources inside child modules. | string | required |\n\nBefore creating a new plan for the resource specified in the `to` field, Terraform checks the state for an existing object at the address specified in the `from` field. Terraform renames existing objects to the string specified in the `to` field and then creates a plan. The plan directs Terraform to provision the resource specified in the `from` field as the resource specified in the `to` field. As a result, Terraform does not destroy the resource during the Terraform run.\n\n## Example\n\nThe following example moves an AWS instance from address `aws_instance.a` to `aws_instance.b`:\n\n```hcl\nmoved {\n  from = aws_instance.a\n  to   = aws_instance.b\n}\n```","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"ceac232f-983d-4180-b05d-2dff83d40a7a","uuid":"8046a362-e9b7-48a1-8429-d45e910baa5b","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"---\npage_title: moved block configuration reference\ndescription: Learn about the `moved` block that you can specify in Terraform configurations. The `moved` block programmatically changes the location of a resource.\n---\n\n# Moved block configuration reference\n\nThis topic provides reference information for the `moved` block. \n\n## Introduction\n\nThe `moved` block programmatically changes the address of a resource. Refer to [Refactoring](/terraform/language/modules/develop/refactoring) for details about how to use the `moved` block in your Terraform configurations.\n\n## Configuration model\n\nThe following list outlines field hierarchy, language-specific data types, and requirements in the `moved` block. \n\n- [`moved`](#moved): map\n  - [`from`](#moved): reference\n  - [`to`](#moved): reference\n\n## Complete configuration\n\nWhen every field is defined, a `moved` block has the following form:\n\n```hcl\nmoved {\n    from = <old address for the resource>\n    to = <new address for the resource>\n}\n```\n\n## Specification\n\nThis section provides details about the fields you can configure in the `moved` block.\n\n### `moved`\n\nMap that specifies addresses for the resource. The following table describes the fields you can set in the `moved` block.\n\n| Field | Description | Type | Required |\n| ---   | ---         | ---  | ---      |\n| `from` | Specifies a resource's previous address. The syntax allows Terraform to select modules, resources, and resources inside child modules. | string | required |\n| `to` | Specifies the new address to relocate the resource to. The syntax allows Terraform to select modules, resources, and resources inside child modules. | string | required |\n\nBefore creating a new plan for the resource specified in the `to` field, Terraform checks the state for an existing object at the address specified in the `from` field. Terraform renames existing objects to the string specified in the `to` field and then creates a plan. The plan directs Terraform to provision the resource specified in the `from` field as the resource specified in the `to` field. As a result, Terraform does not destroy the resource during the Terraform run.\n\n## Example\n\nThe following example moves an AWS instance from address `aws_instance.a` to `aws_instance.b`:\n\n```hcl\nmoved {\n  from = aws_instance.a\n  to   = aws_instance.b\n}\n```","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01YJJDT321Wex2YXZThnbvJw","type":"tool_result","content":"---\npage_title: Ephemeral resource configuration reference\ndescription: Learn about ephemeral resource blocks that you can specify in Terraform configurations. Ephemeral resource blocks represent temporary resources that Terraform does not store in state.\n---\n\n# Ephemeral resource configuration reference\n\nThis topic provides reference information for the `ephemeral` block. \n\n-> **Note**: Ephemeral resources are in public beta and available in Terraform v1.10 and later. The workflows of ephemeral resources are subject to change.\n\n## Introduction\n\nEphemeral resources are Terraform resources that are essentially temporary. Ephemeral resources have a unique lifecycle, and Terraform does not store them in its state. Each `ephemeral` block describes one or more ephemeral resources, such as a temporary password or connection to another system.\n\n<!-- After page exists, add: For information about how Terraform manages ephemeral resources, refer to\n[Epehemeral Values](/terraform/language/epehemeral-values). -->\n\nAn `ephemeral` block declares an ephemeral resource of a specific type with a\nspecific local name, much like a `resource` block. Terraform uses an ephemeral resource's name to\nrefer to that resource in the same module, but an ephemeral resource's name has no meaning\noutside that module's scope.\n\n\n## Lifecycle\n\nThe lifecycle of an ephemeral resource is different from resources and data sources. When Terraform provisions ephemeral resources, it performs the following steps:\n\n1. If Terraform needs to access the result of an ephemeral resource, it opens\nthat ephemeral resource. For example, if Terraform opens an ephemeral resource for a Vault secret, the Vault provider obtains a lease and returns a secret. \n\n1. If Terraform needs access to the ephemeral resource for longer than the\nremote system's enforced expiration time, Terraform asks the provider\nto renew it periodically. For example, if Terraform renews a Vault secret ephemeral resource, the Vault provider then calls Vault's lease renewal API endpoint to extend the expiration time.\n\n1. Once Terraform no longer needs an ephemeral resource, Terraform closes\nit. This happens after the providers that depend on a certain ephemeral resource\ncomplete all of their work for the current Terraform run phase. For example, closing a Vault secret ephemeral resource means the Vault provider explicitly ends the lease, allowing Vault to immediately revoke the associated credentials.\n\nTerraform follows these lifecycle steps for each instance of an ephemeral\nresource in a given configuration.\n\n## Dependency graph\n\nEphemeral resources form nodes in Terraform's dependency graph, which interact similarly as resources and data sources. For example, when a resource or data source depends on an attribute of an ephemeral resource, Terraform automatically provisions the ephemeral resource first.\n\n## Configuration model\n\nMost of the arguments within the body of an `ephemeral` block are specific to the ephemeral resource you are defining. As with resources and data sources, each provider in the [Terraform Registry](https://registry.terraform.io/browse/providers) includes documentation for the ephemeral resources it supports, if any. An ephemeral resource type's documentation lists which arguments are available and how you should format your resource's values. \n\nThe following list outlines general field hierarchy, language-specific data types, and requirements in the `ephemeral` block. \n\n- [`ephemeral`](#ephemeral): map\n  - [`attributes`](#ephemeral)\n  - [`meta-arguments`](#ephemeral)\n\n## Complete configuration\n\nAn `ephemeral` block has the following form:\n\n```hcl\nephemeral \"<resource_type>\" \"<resource_name>\" {\n  <attributes>\n  <meta-arguments>\n}\n```\n\n## Referencing ephemeral resources\n\nYou can only reference ephemeral resources in specific ephemeral contexts or\nTerraform throws an error. The following are valid contexts for referencing\nephemeral resources:\n\n* In another ephemeral resource\n* In [local values](/terraform/language/values/locals#ephemeral-values)\n* In [ephemeral variables](/terraform/language/values/variables#exclude-values-from-state)\n* In [ephemeral outputs](/terraform/language/values/outputs#ephemeral-avoid-storing-values-in-state-or-plan-files)\n* Configuring providers in the `provider` block\n* In [provisioner](/terraform/language/resources/provisioners/syntax) and [connection](/terraform/language/resources/provisioners/connection) blocks\n\n## Meta-arguments\n\nYou can use the following meta-arguments with ephemeral resources to change the behavior of those resources. The following meta-arguments work the same way for resources, data sources, and ephemeral\nresources:\n\n- [`depends_on`, for specifying hidden dependencies](/terraform/language/meta-arguments/depends_on)\n- [`count`, for creating multiple resource instances according to a count](/terraform/language/meta-arguments/count)\n- [`for_each`, to create multiple instances according to a map or set of strings](/terraform/language/meta-arguments/for_each)\n- [`provider`, for selecting a non-default provider configuration](/terraform/language/meta-arguments/resource-provider)\n- [`lifecycle`, for lifecycle customizations](/terraform/language/meta-arguments/lifecycle)\n\nEphemeral resources do not support the `provisioner` meta-argument.\n\n## Example\n\nThe following example configures the `postgresql` provider with credentials from\nan ephemeral resource. Since these credentials are managed by an ephemeral resource, Terraform does not store them in your state or plan files.\n\n```hcl\nephemeral \"aws_secretsmanager_secret_version\" \"db_master\" {\n  secret_id = data.aws_db_instance.example.master_user_secret[0].secret_arn\n}\n\nlocals {\n  credentials = jsondecode(ephemeral.aws_secretsmanager_secret_version.db_master.secret_string)\n}\n\nprovider \"postgresql\" {\n  host     = data.aws_db_instance.example.address\n  port     = data.aws_db_instance.example.port\n  username = local.credentials[\"username\"]\n  password = local.credentials[\"password\"]\n}\n```","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"ceac232f-983d-4180-b05d-2dff83d40a7a","uuid":"f236cb63-82a1-495e-80b1-b605273bfac3","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"---\npage_title: Ephemeral resource configuration reference\ndescription: Learn about ephemeral resource blocks that you can specify in Terraform configurations. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01AJcVydwouvGAcBMzPK5wqE","type":"tool_result","content":"---\npage_title: Input Variables - Configuration Language\ndescription: >-\n  Input variables allow you to customize modules without altering their source\n  code. Learn how to declare, define, and reference variables in configurations.\n---\n\n# Input Variables\n\n> **Hands-on:** Try the [Customize Terraform Configuration with Variables](/terraform/tutorials/configuration-language/variables?utm_source=WEBSITE&utm_medium=WEB_IO&utm_offer=ARTICLE_PAGE&utm_content=DOCS) tutorial.\n\nInput variables let you customize aspects of Terraform modules without altering\nthe module's own source code. This functionality allows you to share modules across different\nTerraform configurations, making your module composable and reusable.\n\nWhen you declare variables in the root module of your configuration, you can\nset their values using CLI options and environment variables.\nWhen you declare them in [child modules](/terraform/language/modules),\nthe calling module should pass values in the `module` block.\n\nIf you're familiar with traditional programming languages, it can be useful to\ncompare Terraform modules to function definitions:\n\n* Input variables are like function arguments.\n* [Output values](/terraform/language/values/outputs) are like function return values.\n* [Local values](/terraform/language/values/locals) are like a function's temporary local variables.\n\n-> **Note:** For brevity, input variables are often referred to as just\n\"variables\" or \"Terraform variables\" when it is clear from context what sort of\nvariable is being discussed. Other kinds of variables in Terraform include\n_environment variables_ (set by the shell where Terraform runs) and _expression\nvariables_ (used to indirectly represent a value in an\n[expression](/terraform/language/expressions)).\n\n## Declaring an Input Variable\n\nEach input variable accepted by a module must be declared using a `variable`\nblock:\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"image_id\" {\n  type = string\n}\n\nvariable \"availability_zone_names\" {\n  type    = list(string)\n  default = [\"us-west-1a\"]\n}\n\nvariable \"docker_ports\" {\n  type = list(object({\n    internal = number\n    external = number\n    protocol = string\n  }))\n  default = [\n    {\n      internal = 8300\n      external = 8300\n      protocol = \"tcp\"\n    }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\nThe label after the `variable` keyword is a name for the variable, which must\nbe unique among all variables in the same module. This name is used to\nassign a value to the variable from outside and to reference the variable's\nvalue from within the module.\n\nThe name of a variable can be any valid [identifier](/terraform/language/syntax/configuration#identifiers)\n_except_ the following: `source`, `version`, `providers`, `count`, `for_each`, `lifecycle`, `depends_on`, `locals`.\n\nThese names are reserved for meta-arguments in\n[module configuration blocks](/terraform/language/modules/syntax), and cannot be\ndeclared as variable names.\n\n## Arguments\n\nTerraform CLI defines the following optional arguments for variable declarations:\n\n* [`default`][inpage-default] - A default value which then makes the variable optional.\n* [`type`][inpage-type] - This argument specifies what value types are accepted for the variable.\n* [`description`][inpage-description] - This specifies the input variable's documentation.\n* [`validation`][inpage-validation] - A block to define validation rules, usually in addition to type constraints.\n* [`ephemeral`][inpage-ephemeral] - This variable is available during runtime, but not written to state or plan files.\n* [`sensitive`][inpage-sensitive] - Limits Terraform UI output when the variable is used in configuration.\n* [`nullable`][inpage-nullable] - Specify if the variable can be `null` within the module.\n\n### Default values\n\n[inpage-default]: #default-values\n\nThe variable declaration can also include a `default` argument. If present,\nthe variable is considered to be _optional_ and the default value will be used\nif no value is set when calling the module or running Terraform. The `default`\nargument requires a literal value and cannot reference other objects in the\nconfiguration.\n\n### Type Constraints\n\n[inpage-type]: #type-constraints\n\nThe `type` argument in a `variable` block allows you to restrict the\n[type of value](/terraform/language/expressions/types) that will be accepted as\nthe value for a variable. If no type constraint is set then a value of any type\nis accepted.\n\nWhile type constraints are optional, we recommend specifying them; they\ncan serve as helpful reminders for users of the module, and they\nallow Terraform to return a helpful error message if the wrong type is used.\n\nType constraints are created from a mixture of type keywords and type\nconstructors. The supported type keywords are:\n\n* `string`\n* `number`\n* `bool`\n\nThe type constructors allow you to specify complex types such as\ncollections:\n\n* `list(<TYPE>)`\n* `set(<TYPE>)`\n* `map(<TYPE>)`\n* `object({<ATTR NAME> = <TYPE>, ... })`\n* `tuple([<TYPE>, ...])`\n\nThe keyword `any` may be used to indicate that any type is acceptable. For\nmore information on the meaning and behavior of these different types, as well\nas detailed information about automatic conversion of complex types, see\n[Type Constraints](/terraform/language/expressions/types).\n\nIf both the `type` and `default` arguments are specified, the given default\nvalue must be convertible to the specified type.\n\n### Input Variable Documentation\n\n[inpage-description]: #input-variable-documentation\n\nBecause the input variables of a module are part of its user interface, you can\nbriefly describe the purpose of each variable using the optional\n`description` argument:\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"image_id\" {\n  type        = string\n  description = \"The id of the machine image (AMI) to use for the server.\"\n}\n```\n\nThe description should concisely explain the purpose\nof the variable and what kind of value is expected. This description string\nmight be included in documentation about the module, and so it should be written\nfrom the perspective of the user of the module rather than its maintainer. For\ncommentary for module maintainers, use comments.\n\n### Custom Validation Rules\n\n[inpage-validation]: #custom-validation-rules\n\n-> This feature was introduced in Terraform CLI v0.13.0.\n\nYou can specify custom validation rules for a particular variable by adding a `validation` block within the corresponding `variable` block. The example below checks whether the AMI ID has the correct syntax.\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"image_id\" {\n  type        = string\n  description = \"The id of the machine image (AMI) to use for the server.\"\n\n  validation {\n    condition     = length(var.image_id) > 4 && substr(var.image_id, 0, 4) == \"ami-\"\n    error_message = \"The image_id value must be a valid AMI id, starting with \\\"ami-\\\".\"\n  }\n}\n```\nRefer to [Custom Condition Checks](/terraform/language/expressions/custom-conditions#input-variable-validation) for more details.\n\n### Exclude values from state\n\n-> **Note**: Ephemeral variables are in public beta and available in Terraform v1.10 and later. The workflows of ephemeral variables are subject to change.\n\n[inpage-ephemeral]: #exclude-values-from-state\n\nSetting a variable as `ephemeral` makes it available during runtime, but Terraform omits ephemeral values from state and plan files. Marking an input variable as `ephemeral` is useful for data that only needs to exist temporarily, such as a short-lived token or session identifier.\n\nMark an input variable as ephemeral by setting the `ephemeral` argument to `true`:\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"session_token\" {\n  type      = string\n  ephemeral = true\n}\n```\n\nEphemeral variables are available during the current Terraform operation, and Terraform does not store them in state or plan files. So, unlike [`sensitive`](#sensitive) inputs, Terraform ensures ephemeral values are not available beyond the lifetime of the current Terraform run.\n\nYou can only reference ephemeral variables in specific contexts or Terraform throws an error. The following are valid contexts for referencing ephemeral variables:\n* Another ephemeral variable\n* In [local values](/terraform/language/values/locals#ephemeral-values)","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"ceac232f-983d-4180-b05d-2dff83d40a7a","uuid":"12001aac-d0a0-4ddc-8606-c39b04dd4393","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"---\npage_title: Input Variables - Configuration Language\ndescription: >-\n  Input variables allow you to customize modules without altering their source\n  code. Learn how to declare, define, and reference variables in configurations.\n---\n\n# Input Variables\n\n> **Hands-on:** Try the [Customize Terraform Configuration with Variables](/terraform/tutorials/configuration-language/variables?utm_source=WEBSITE&utm_medium=WEB_IO&utm_offer=ARTICLE_PAGE&utm_content=DOCS) tutorial.\n\nInput variables let you customize aspects of Terraform modules without altering\nthe module's own source code. This functionality allows you to share modules across different\nTerraform configurations, making your module composable and reusable.\n\nWhen you declare variables in the root module of your configuration, you can\nset their values using CLI options and environment variables.\nWhen you declare them in [child modules](/terraform/language/modules),\nthe calling module should pass values in the `module` block.\n\nIf you're familiar with traditional programming languages, it can be useful to\ncompare Terraform modules to function definitions:\n\n* Input variables are like function arguments.\n* [Output values](/terraform/language/values/outputs) are like function return values.\n* [Local values](/terraform/language/values/locals) are like a function's temporary local variables.\n\n-> **Note:** For brevity, input variables are often referred to as just\n\"variables\" or \"Terraform variables\" when it is clear from context what sort of\nvariable is being discussed. Other kinds of variables in Terraform include\n_environment variables_ (set by the shell where Terraform runs) and _expression\nvariables_ (used to indirectly represent a value in an\n[expression](/terraform/language/expressions)).\n\n## Declaring an Input Variable\n\nEach input variable accepted by a module must be declared using a `variable`\nblock:\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"image_id\" {\n  type = string\n}\n\nvariable \"availability_zone_names\" {\n  type    = list(string)\n  default = [\"us-west-1a\"]\n}\n\nvariable \"docker_ports\" {\n  type = list(object({\n    internal = number\n    external = number\n    protocol = string\n  }))\n  default = [\n    {\n      internal = 8300\n      external = 8300\n      protocol = \"tcp\"\n    }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\nThe label after the `variable` keyword is a name for the variable, which must\nbe unique among all variables in the same module. This name is used to\nassign a value to the variable from outside and to reference the variable's\nvalue from within the module.\n\nThe name of a variable can be any valid [identifier](/terraform/language/syntax/configuration#identifiers)\n_except_ the following: `source`, `version`, `providers`, `count`, `for_each`, `lifecycle`, `depends_on`, `locals`.\n\nThese names are reserved for meta-arguments in\n[module configuration blocks](/terraform/language/modules/syntax), and cannot be\ndeclared as variable names.\n\n## Arguments\n\nTerraform CLI defines the following optional arguments for variable declarations:\n\n* [`default`][inpage-default] - A default value which then makes the variable optional.\n* [`type`][inpage-type] - This argument specifies what value types are accepted for the variable.\n* [`description`][inpage-description] - This specifies the input variable's documentation.\n* [`validation`][inpage-validation] - A block to define validation rules, usually in addition to type constraints.\n* [`ephemeral`][inpage-ephemeral] - This variable is available during runtime, but not written to state or plan files.\n* [`sensitive`][inpage-sensitive] - Limits Terraform UI output when the variable is used in configuration.\n* [`nullable`][inpage-nullable] - Specify if the variable can be `null` within the module.\n\n### Default values\n\n[inpage-default]: #default-values\n\nThe variable declaration can also include a `default` argument. If present,\nthe variable is considered to be _optional_ and the default value will be used\nif no value is set when calling the module or running Terraform. The `default`\nargument requires a literal value and cannot reference other objects in the\nconfiguration.\n\n### Type Constraints\n\n[inpage-type]: #type-constraints\n\nThe `type` argument in a `variable` block allows you to restrict the\n[type of value](/terraform/language/expressions/types) that will be accepted as\nthe value for a variable. If no type constraint is set then a value of any type\nis accepted.\n\nWhile type constraints are optional, we recommend specifying them; they\ncan serve as helpful reminders for users of the module, and they\nallow Terraform to return a helpful error message if the wrong type is used.\n\nType constraints are created from a mixture of type keywords and type\nconstructors. The supported type keywords are:\n\n* `string`\n* `number`\n* `bool`\n\nThe type constructors allow you to specify complex types such as\ncollections:\n\n* `list(<TYPE>)`\n* `set(<TYPE>)`\n* `map(<TYPE>)`\n* `object({<ATTR NAME> = <TYPE>, ... })`\n* `tuple([<TYPE>, ...])`\n\nThe keyword `any` may be used to indicate that any type is acceptable. For\nmore information on the meaning and behavior of these different types, as well\nas detailed information about automatic conversion of complex types, see\n[Type Constraints](/terraform/language/expressions/types).\n\nIf both the `type` and `default` arguments are specified, the given default\nvalue must be convertible to the specified type.\n\n### Input Variable Documentation\n\n[inpage-description]: #input-variable-documentation\n\nBecause the input variables of a module are part of its user interface, you can\nbriefly describe the purpose of each variable using the optional\n`description` argument:\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"image_id\" {\n  type        = string\n  description = \"The id of the machine image (AMI) to use for the server.\"\n}\n```\n\nThe description should concisely explain the purpose\nof the variable and what kind of value is expected. This description string\nmight be included in documentation about the module, and so it should be written\nfrom the perspective of the user of the module rather than its maintainer. For\ncommentary for module maintainers, use comments.\n\n### Custom Validation Rules\n\n[inpage-validation]: #custom-validation-rules\n\n-> This feature was introduced in Terraform CLI v0.13.0.\n\nYou can specify custom validation rules for a particular variable by adding a `validation` block within the corresponding `variable` block. The example below checks whether the AMI ID has the correct syntax.\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"image_id\" {\n  type        = string\n  description = \"The id of the machine image (AMI) to use for the server.\"\n\n  validation {\n    condition     = length(var.image_id) > 4 && substr(var.image_id, 0, 4) == \"ami-\"\n    error_message = \"The image_id value must be a valid AMI id, starting with \\\"ami-\\\".\"\n  }\n}\n```\nRefer to [Custom Condition Checks](/terraform/language/expressions/custom-conditions#input-variable-validation) for more details.\n\n### Exclude values from state\n\n-> **Note**: Ephemeral variables are in public beta and available in Terraform v1.10 and later. The workflows of ephemeral variables are subject to change.\n\n[inpage-ephemeral]: #exclude-values-from-state\n\nSetting a variable as `ephemeral` makes it available during runtime, but Terraform omits ephemeral values from state and plan files. Marking an input variable as `ephemeral` is useful for data that only needs to exist temporarily, such as a short-lived token or session identifier.\n\nMark an input variable as ephemeral by setting the `ephemeral` argument to `true`:\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"session_token\" {\n  type      = string\n  ephemeral = true\n}\n```\n\nEphemeral variables are available during the current Terraform operation, and Terraform does not store them in state or plan files. So, unlike [`sensitive`](#sensitive) inputs, Terraform ensures ephemeral values are not available beyond the lifetime of the current Terraform run.\n\nYou can only reference ephemeral variables in specific contexts or Terraform throws an error. The following are valid contexts for referencing ephemeral variables:\n* Another ephemeral variable\n* In [local values](/terraform/language/values/locals#ephemeral-values)","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01VfSrG5Z91RZFojs8hcM9NY","type":"tool_result","content":"---\npage_title: Conditional Expressions - Configuration Language\ndescription: >-\n  Conditional expressions select one of two values. You can use them to define\n  defaults to replace invalid values.\n---\n\n# Conditional Expressions\n\nA _conditional expression_ uses the value of a boolean expression to select one of\ntwo values.\n\n> **Hands-on:** Try the [Create Dynamic Expressions](/terraform/tutorials/configuration-language/expressions?utm_source=WEBSITE&utm_medium=WEB_IO&utm_offer=ARTICLE_PAGE&utm_content=DOCS) tutorial.\n\n## Syntax\n\nThe syntax of a conditional expression is as follows:\n\n```hcl\ncondition ? true_val : false_val\n```\n\nIf `condition` is `true` then the result is `true_val`. If `condition` is\n`false` then the result is `false_val`.\n\nA common use of conditional expressions is to define defaults to replace\ninvalid values:\n\n```hcl\nvar.a != \"\" ? var.a : \"default-a\"\n```\n\nIf `var.a` is an empty string then the result is `\"default-a\"`, but otherwise\nit is the actual value of `var.a`.\n\n## Conditions\n\nThe condition can be any expression that resolves to a boolean value. This will\nusually be an expression that uses the equality, comparison, or logical\noperators.\n\n### Custom Condition Checks\n\nYou can create conditions that produce custom error messages for several types of objects in a configuration. For example, you can add a condition to an input variable that checks whether incoming image IDs are formatted properly.\n\nCustom conditions can help capture assumptions, helping future maintainers understand the configuration design and intent. They also return useful information about errors earlier and in context, helping consumers more easily diagnose issues in their configurations.\n\nRefer to [Custom Condition Checks](/terraform/language/expressions/custom-conditions#input-variable-validation) for details.\n\n## Result Types\n\nThe two result values may be of any type, but they must both\nbe of the _same_ type so that Terraform can determine what type the whole\nconditional expression will return without knowing the condition value.\n\nIf the two result expressions don't produce the same type then Terraform will\nattempt to find a type that they can both convert to, and make those\nconversions automatically if so.\n\nFor example, the following expression is valid and will always return a string,\nbecause in Terraform all numbers can convert automatically to a string using\ndecimal digits:\n\n```hcl\nvar.example ? 12 : \"hello\"\n```\n\nRelying on this automatic conversion behavior can be confusing for those who\nare not familiar with Terraform's conversion rules though, so we recommend\nbeing explicit using type conversion functions in any situation where there may\nbe some uncertainty about the expected result type.\n\nThe following example is contrived because it would be easier to write the\nconstant `\"12\"` instead of the type conversion in this case, but shows how to\nuse [`tostring`](/terraform/language/functions/tostring) to explicitly convert a number to\na string.\n\n```hcl\nvar.example ? tostring(12) : \"hello\"\n```","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"ceac232f-983d-4180-b05d-2dff83d40a7a","uuid":"18293346-32b0-4ab9-81ad-bd4074d470a1","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"---\npage_title: Conditional Expressions - Configuration Language\ndescription: >-\n  Conditional expressions select one of two values. If `condition` is\n`false` then the result is `false_val`.\n\nA common use of conditional expressions is to define defaults to replace\ninvalid values:\n\n```hcl\nvar.a != \"\" ? var.a : \"default-a\"\n```\n\nIf `var.a` is an empty string then the result is `\"default-a\"`, but otherwise\nit is the actual value of `var.a`.\n\n## Conditions\n\nThe condition can be any expression that resolves to a boolean value. This will\nusually be an expression that uses the equality, comparison, or logical\noperators.\n\n### Custom Condition Checks\n\nYou can create conditions that produce custom error messages for several types of objects in a configuration. For example, you can add a condition to an input variable that checks whether incoming image IDs are formatted properly.\n\nCustom conditions can help capture assumptions, helping future maintainers understand the configuration design and intent. They also return useful information about errors earlier and in context, helping consumers more easily diagnose issues in their configurations.\n\nRefer to [Custom Condition Checks](/terraform/language/expressions/custom-conditions#input-variable-validation) for details.\n\n## Result Types\n\nThe two result values may be of any type, but they must both\nbe of the _same_ type so that Terraform can determine what type the whole\nconditional expression will return without knowing the condition value.\n\nIf the two result expressions don't produce the same type then Terraform will\nattempt to find a type that they can both convert to, and make those\nconversions automatically if so.\n\nFor example, the following expression is valid and will always return a string,\nbecause in Terraform all numbers can convert automatically to a string using\ndecimal digits:\n\n```hcl\nvar.example ? 12 : \"hello\"\n```\n\nRelying on this automatic conversion behavior can be confusing for those who\nare not familiar with Terraform's conversion rules though, so we recommend\nbeing explicit using type conversion functions in any situation where there may\nbe some uncertainty about the expected result type.\n\nThe following example is contrived because it would be easier to write the\nconstant `\"12\"` instead of the type conversion in this case, but shows how to\nuse [`tostring`](/terraform/language/functions/tostring) to explicitly convert a number to\na string.\n\n```hcl\nvar.example ? tostring(12) : \"hello\"\n```","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_011aAHhAMNoYqsC8zUbpS5iM","type":"tool_result","content":"---\npage_title: 'Backend Type: s3'\ndescription: Terraform can store state remotely in S3 and lock that state with DynamoDB.\n---\n\n# S3\n\nStores the state as a given key in a given bucket on [Amazon S3](https://aws.amazon.com/s3/).\nThis backend also supports state locking and consistency checking via [Dynamo DB](https://aws.amazon.com/dynamodb/), which can be enabled by setting the `dynamodb_table` field to an existing DynamoDB table name.\nA single DynamoDB table can be used to lock multiple remote state files.\nTerraform generates key names that include the values of the `bucket` and `key` variables.\n\n~> **Warning!** It is highly recommended that you enable\n[Bucket Versioning](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/manage-versioning-examples.html)\non the S3 bucket to allow for state recovery in the case of accidental deletions and human error.\n\n## Example Configuration\n\n```hcl\nterraform {\n  backend \"s3\" {\n    bucket = \"example-bucket\"\n    key    = \"path/to/state\"\n    region = \"us-east-1\"\n  }\n}\n```\n\nThis assumes we have a bucket created called `mybucket`. The Terraform state is written to the key `path/to/my/key`.\n\nNote that for the access credentials we recommend using a [partial configuration](/terraform/language/backend#partial-configuration).\n\n## State Storage\n\nThe S3 backend stores state data in an S3 object at the path set by the `key` parameter in the S3 bucket indicated by the `bucket` parameter.\nUsing the example shown above, the state would be stored at the path `path/to/my/key` in the bucket `mybucket`.\n\nWhen using [workspaces](/terraform/language/state/workspaces), the state for the `default` workspace is stored at the location described above.\nOther workspaces are stored using the path `<workspace_key_prefix>/<workspace_name>/<key>`.\nThe default workspace key prefix is `env:` and it can be configured using the parameter `workspace_key_prefix`.\nUsing the example above, the state for the workspace `development` would be stored at the path `env:/development/path/to/my/key`.\n\n## Permissions Required\n\n### S3 Bucket Permissions\n\nWhen not using [workspaces](/terraform/language/state/workspaces)(or when only using the `default` workspace), Terraform will need the following AWS IAM permissions on the target backend bucket:\n\n* `s3:ListBucket` on `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket`. At a minimum, this must be able to list the path where the state is stored.\n* `s3:GetObject` on `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/path/to/my/key`\n* `s3:PutObject` on `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/path/to/my/key`\n\nNote: `s3:DeleteObject` is not needed, as Terraform will not delete the state storage.\n\nThis is seen in the following AWS IAM Statement:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"Version\": \"2012-10-17\",\n  \"Statement\": [\n    {\n      \"Effect\": \"Allow\",\n      \"Action\": \"s3:ListBucket\",\n      \"Resource\": \"arn:aws:s3:::mybucket\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"Effect\": \"Allow\",\n      \"Action\": [\"s3:GetObject\", \"s3:PutObject\"],\n      \"Resource\": \"arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/path/to/my/key\"\n    }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\nWhen using [workspaces](/terraform/language/state/workspaces), Terraform will also need permissions to create, list, read, update, and delete the workspace state storage:\n\n* `s3:ListBucket` on `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket`. At a minumum, this must be able to list the path where the `default` workspace is stored as well as the other workspaces.\n* `s3:GetObject` on `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/path/to/my/key` and `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/<workspace_key_prefix>/*/path/to/my/key`\n* `s3:PutObject` on `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/path/to/my/key` and `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/<workspace_key_prefix>/*/path/to/my/key`\n* `s3:DeleteObject` on `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/<workspace_key_prefix>/*/path/to/my/key`\n\n-> **Note:** AWS can control access to S3 buckets with either IAM policies\nattached to users/groups/roles (like the example above) or resource policies\nattached to bucket objects (which look similar but also require a `Principal` to\nindicate which entity has those permissions). For more details, see Amazon's\ndocumentation about\n[S3 access control](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/s3-access-control.html).\n\n### DynamoDB Table Permissions\n\nIf you are using state locking, Terraform will need the following AWS IAM\npermissions on the DynamoDB table (`arn:aws:dynamodb:::table/mytable`):\n\n* `dynamodb:DescribeTable`\n* `dynamodb:GetItem`\n* `dynamodb:PutItem`\n* `dynamodb:DeleteItem`\n\nThis is seen in the following AWS IAM Statement:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"Version\": \"2012-10-17\",\n  \"Statement\": [\n    {\n      \"Effect\": \"Allow\",\n      \"Action\": [\n        \"dynamodb:DescribeTable\",\n        \"dynamodb:GetItem\",\n        \"dynamodb:PutItem\",\n        \"dynamodb:DeleteItem\"\n      ],\n      \"Resource\": \"arn:aws:dynamodb:*:*:table/mytable\"\n    }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\n## Data Source Configuration\n\nTo make use of the S3 remote state in another configuration,\nuse the [`terraform_remote_state` data source](/terraform/language/state/remote-state-data).\n\n```hcl\ndata \"terraform_remote_state\" \"network\" {\n  backend = \"s3\"\n  config = {\n    bucket = \"terraform-state-prod\"\n    key    = \"network/terraform.tfstate\"\n    region = \"us-east-1\"\n  }\n}\n```\n\nThe `terraform_remote_state` data source will return all of the root module\noutputs defined in the referenced remote state (but not any outputs from\nnested modules unless they are explicitly output again in the root). An\nexample output might look like:\n\n```\ndata.terraform_remote_state.network:\n  id = 2016-10-29 01:57:59.780010914 +0000 UTC\n  addresses.# = 2\n  addresses.0 = 52.207.220.222\n  addresses.1 = 54.196.78.166\n  backend = s3\n  config.% = 3\n  config.bucket = terraform-state-prod\n  config.key = network/terraform.tfstate\n  config.region = us-east-1\n  elb_address = web-elb-790251200.us-east-1.elb.amazonaws.com\n  public_subnet_id = subnet-1e05dd33\n```\n\n## Configuration\n\nThis backend requires the configuration of the AWS Region and S3 state storage. Other configuration, such as enabling DynamoDB state locking, is optional.\n\n### Credentials and Shared Configuration\n\n!> **Warning:**  We recommend using environment variables to supply credentials and other sensitive data. If you use `-backend-config` or hardcode these values directly in your configuration, Terraform will include these values in both the `.terraform` subdirectory and in plan files. Refer to [Credentials and Sensitive Data](/terraform/language/backend#credentials-and-sensitive-data) for details.\n\nThe following configuration is required:\n\n* `region` - (Required) AWS Region of the S3 Bucket and DynamoDB Table (if used). This can also be sourced from the `AWS_DEFAULT_REGION` and `AWS_REGION` environment variables.\n\nThe following configuration is optional:\n\n* `use_lockfile` - (Experimental, Optional) Whether to use a lockfile for locking the state file. Defaults to `false`.\n* `access_key` - (Optional) AWS access key. If configured, must also configure `secret_key`. This can also be sourced from the `AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID` environment variable, AWS shared credentials file (e.g. `~/.aws/credentials`), or AWS shared configuration file (e.g. `~/.aws/config`).\n* `allowed_account_ids` - (Optional) List of allowed AWS account IDs to prevent potential destruction of a live environment. Conflicts with `forbidden_account_ids`.\n* `custom_ca_bundle` - (Optional) File containing custom root and intermediate certificates. Can also be set using the `AWS_CA_BUNDLE` environment variable. Setting ca_bundle in the shared config file is not supported.\n* `ec2_metadata_service_endpoint` - (Optional) Custom endpoint URL for the EC2 Instance Metadata Service (IMDS) API.\n  Can also be set with the `AWS_EC2_METADATA_SERVICE_ENDPOINT` environment variable.\n* `ec2_metadata_service_endpoint_mode` - (Optional) Mode to use in communicating with the metadata service.\n  Valid values are `IPv4` and `IPv6`.\n  Can also be set with the `AWS_EC2_METADATA_SERVICE_ENDPOINT_MODE` environment variable.\n* `forbidden_account_ids` - (Optional) List of forbidden AWS account IDs to prevent potential destruction of a live environment. Conflicts with `allowed_account_ids`.\n* `http_proxy` - (Optional) URL of a proxy to use for HTTP requests when accessing the AWS API.\n  Can also be set using the `HTTP_PROXY` or `http_proxy` environment variables.\n* `https_proxy` - (Optional) URL of a proxy to use for HTTPS requests when accessing the AWS API.\n  Can also be set using the `HTTPS_PROXY` or `https_proxy` environment variables.\n* `iam_endpoint` - (Optional, **Deprecated**) Custom endpoint URL for the AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) API.\n  Use `endpoints.iam` instead.\n* `insecure` - (Optional) Whether to explicitly allow the backend to perform \"insecure\" SSL requests. If omitted, the default value is `false`.\n* `no_proxy` - (Optional) Comma-separated list of hosts that should not use HTTP or HTTPS proxies.\n  Each value can be one of:\n    * A domain name\n    * An IP address\n    * A CIDR address\n    * An asterisk (`*`), to indicate that no proxying should be performed\n  Domain name and IP address values can also include a port number.\n  Can also be set using the `NO_PROXY` or `no_proxy` environment variables.\n* `max_retries` - (Optional) The maximum number of times an AWS API request is retried on retryable failure. Defaults to 5.\n* `profile` - (Optional) Name of AWS profile in AWS shared credentials file (e.g. `~/.aws/credentials`) or AWS shared configuration file (e.g. `~/.aws/config`) to use for credentials and/or configuration. This can also be sourced from the `AWS_PROFILE` environment variable.\n* `retry_mode` - (Optional) Specifies how retries are attempted. Valid values are `standard` and `adaptive`. Can also be configured using the `AWS_RETRY_MODE` environment variable or the shared config file parameter `retry_mode`.\n* `secret_key` - (Optional) AWS access key. If configured, must also configure `access_key`. This can also be sourced from the `AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY` environment variable, AWS shared credentials file (e.g. `~/.aws/credentials`), or AWS shared configuration file (e.g. `~/.aws/config`).\n* `shared_config_files`  - (Optional) List of paths to AWS shared configuration files. Defaults to `~/.aws/config`.\n* `shared_credentials_file`  - (Optional, **Deprecated**, use `shared_credentials_files` instead) Path to the AWS shared credentials file. Defaults to `~/.aws/credentials`.\n* `shared_credentials_files`  - (Optional) List of paths to AWS shared credentials files. Defaults to `~/.aws/credentials`.\n* `skip_credentials_validation` - (Optional) Skip credentials validation via the STS API.\n  Useful for testing and for AWS API implementations that do not have STS available.\n* `skip_region_validation` - (Optional) Skip validation of provided region name.\n* `skip_requesting_account_id` - (Optional) Whether to skip requesting the account ID.\n  Useful for AWS API implementations that do not have the IAM, STS API, or metadata API.\n* `skip_metadata_api_check` - (Optional) Skip usage of EC2 Metadata API.\n* `skip_s3_checksum` - (Optional) Do not include checksum when uploading S3 Objects.\n  Useful for some S3-Compatible APIs.\n* `sts_endpoint` - (Optional, **Deprecated**) Custom endpoint URL for the AWS Security Token Service (STS) API.\n  Use `endpoints.sts` instead.\n* `sts_region` - (Optional) AWS region for STS. If unset, AWS will use the same region for STS as other non-STS operations.\n* `token` - (Optional) Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA) token. This can also be sourced from the `AWS_SESSION_TOKEN` environment variable.\n* `use_dualstack_endpoint` - (Optional) Force the backend to resolve endpoints with DualStack capability. Can also be set with the `AWS_USE_DUALSTACK_ENDPOINT` environment variable or in a shared config file (`use_dualstack_endpoint`).\n* `use_fips_endpoint` - (Optional) Force the backend to resolve endpoints with FIPS capability. Can also be set with the `AWS_USE_FIPS_ENDPOINT` environment variable or in a shared config file (`use_fips_endpoint`).\n\n#### Overriding AWS API endpoints\n\nThe optional argument `endpoints` contains the following arguments:\n\n* `dynamodb` - (Optional) Custom endpoint URL for the AWS DynamoDB API.\n  This can also be sourced from the environment variable `AWS_ENDPOINT_URL_DYNAMODB` or the deprecated environment variable `AWS_DYNAMODB_ENDPOINT`.\n* `iam` - (Optional) Custom endpoint URL for the AWS IAM API.\n  This can also be sourced from the environment variable `AWS_ENDPOINT_URL_IAM` or the deprecated environment variable `AWS_IAM_ENDPOINT`.\n* `s3` - (Optional) Custom endpoint URL for the AWS S3 API.\n  This can also be sourced from the environment variable `AWS_ENDPOINT_URL_S3` or the deprecated environment variable `AWS_S3_ENDPOINT`.\n* `sso` - (Optional) Custom endpoint URL for the AWS IAM Identity Center (formerly known as AWS SSO) API.\n  This can also be sourced from the environment variable `AWS_ENDPOINT_URL_SSO`.\n* `sts` - (Optional) Custom endpoint URL for the AWS STS API.\n  This can also be sourced from the environment variable `AWS_ENDPOINT_URL_STS` or the deprecated environment variable `AWS_STS_ENDPOINT`.\n\nThe environment variable `AWS_ENDPOINT_URL` can be used to set a base endpoint URL for all services.\n\nEndpoints can also be overridden using the [AWS shared configuration file](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sdkref/latest/guide/feature-ss-endpoints.html#ss-endpoints-config).\nSetting the parameter `endpoint_url` on a profile will set that endpoint for all services.\nTo set endpoints for specific services, create a `services` section and set the `endpoint_url` parameters for each desired service.\nEndpoints set for specific services will override the base endpoint configured in the profile.\n\n#### Assume Role Configuration\n\nThe argument `assume_role` contains the following arguments:\n\n* `role_arn` - (Required) Amazon Resource Name (ARN) of the IAM Role to assume.\n* `duration` - (Optional) The duration individual credentials will be valid.\n  Credentials are automatically renewed up to the maximum defined by the AWS account.\n  Specified using the format `<hours>h<minutes>m<seconds>s` with any unit being optional.\n  For example, an hour and a half can be specified as `1h30m` or `90m`. \n  Must be between 15 minutes (15m) and 12 hours (12h).\n* `external_id` - (Optional) External identifier to use when assuming the role.\n* `policy` - (Optional) IAM Policy JSON describing further restricting permissions for the IAM Role being assumed.\n* `policy_arns` - (Optional) Set of Amazon Resource Names (ARNs) of IAM Policies describing further restricting permissions for the IAM Role being assumed.\n* `session_name` - (Optional) Session name to use when assuming the role.\n* `source_identity` - (Optional) Source identity specified by the principal assuming the role.\n* `tags` - (Optional) Map of assume role session tags.\n* `transitive_tag_keys` - (Optional) Set of assume role session tag keys to pass to any subsequent sessions.\n\nMultiple `assume_role` values can be specified, and the roles will be assumed in order.\n\n```terraform\nterraform {\n  backend \"s3\" {\n    bucket = \"example-bucket\"\n    key    = \"path/to/state\"\n    region = \"us-east-1\"\n    assume_role = {\n      role_arn = \"arn:aws:iam::PRODUCTION-ACCOUNT-ID:role/Terraform\"\n    }\n  }\n}\n```\n\n#### Assume Role With Web Identity Configuration\n\nThe following `assume_role_with_web_identity` configuration block is optional:\n\n* `role_arn` - The Terraform state is written to the key `path/to/my/key`.\n\nNote that for the access credentials we recommend using a [partial configuration](/terraform/language/backend#partial-configuration).\n\n## State Storage\n\nThe S3 backend stores state data in an S3 object at the path set by the `key` parameter in the S3 bucket indicated by the `bucket` parameter.\nUsing the example shown above, the state would be stored at the path `path/to/my/key` in the bucket `mybucket`.\n\nWhen using [workspaces](/terraform/language/state/workspaces), the state for the `default` workspace is stored at the location described above.\nOther workspaces are stored using the path `<workspace_key_prefix>/<workspace_name>/<key>`.\nThe default workspace key prefix is `env:` and it can be configured using the parameter `workspace_key_prefix`.\nUsing the example above, the state for the workspace `development` would be stored at the path `env:/development/path/to/my/key`.\n\n## Permissions Required\n\n### S3 Bucket Permissions\n\nWhen not using [workspaces](/terraform/language/state/workspaces)(or when only using the `default` workspace), Terraform will need the following AWS IAM permissions on the target backend bucket:\n\n* `s3:ListBucket` on `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket`. At a minimum, this must be able to list the path where the state is stored.\n* `s3:GetObject` on `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/path/to/my/key`\n* `s3:PutObject` on `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/path/to/my/key`\n\nNote: `s3:DeleteObject` is not needed, as Terraform will not delete the state storage.\n\nThis is seen in the following AWS IAM Statement:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"Version\": \"2012-10-17\",\n  \"Statement\": [\n    {\n      \"Effect\": \"Allow\",\n      \"Action\": \"s3:ListBucket\",\n      \"Resource\": \"arn:aws:s3:::mybucket\"\n    },\n    {\n      \"Effect\": \"Allow\",\n      \"Action\": [\"s3:GetObject\", \"s3:PutObject\"],\n      \"Resource\": \"arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/path/to/my/key\"\n    }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\nWhen using [workspaces](/terraform/language/state/workspaces), Terraform will also need permissions to create, list, read, update, and delete the workspace state storage:\n\n* `s3:ListBucket` on `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket`. At a minumum, this must be able to list the path where the `default` workspace is stored as well as the other workspaces.\n* `s3:GetObject` on `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/path/to/my/key` and `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/<workspace_key_prefix>/*/path/to/my/key`\n* `s3:PutObject` on `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/path/to/my/key` and `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/<workspace_key_prefix>/*/path/to/my/key`\n* `s3:DeleteObject` on `arn:aws:s3:::mybucket/<workspace_key_prefix>/*/path/to/my/key`\n\n-> **Note:** AWS can control access to S3 buckets with either IAM policies\nattached to users/groups/roles (like the example above) or resource policies\nattached to bucket objects (which look similar but also require a `Principal` to\nindicate which entity has those permissions). For more details, see Amazon's\ndocumentation about\n[S3 access control](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/s3-access-control.html).\n\n### DynamoDB Table Permissions\n\nIf you are using state locking, Terraform will need the following AWS IAM\npermissions on the DynamoDB table (`arn:aws:dynamodb:::table/mytable`):\n\n* `dynamodb:DescribeTable`\n* `dynamodb:GetItem`\n* `dynamodb:PutItem`\n* `dynamodb:DeleteItem`\n\nThis is seen in the following AWS IAM Statement:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"Version\": \"2012-10-17\",\n  \"Statement\": [\n    {\n      \"Effect\": \"Allow\",\n      \"Action\": [\n        \"dynamodb:DescribeTable\",\n        \"dynamodb:GetItem\",\n        \"dynamodb:PutItem\",\n        \"dynamodb:DeleteItem\"\n      ],\n      \"Resource\": \"arn:aws:dynamodb:*:*:table/mytable\"\n    }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\n## Data Source Configuration\n\nTo make use of the S3 remote state in another configuration,\nuse the [`terraform_remote_state` data source](/terraform/language/state/remote-state-data).\n\n```hcl\ndata \"terraform_remote_state\" \"network\" {\n  backend = \"s3\"\n  config = {\n    bucket = \"terraform-state-prod\"\n    key    = \"network/terraform.tfstate\"\n    region = \"us-east-1\"\n  }\n}\n```\n\nThe `terraform_remote_state` data source will return all of the root module\noutputs defined in the referenced remote state (but not any outputs from\nnested modules unless they are explicitly output again in the root). An\nexample output might look like:\n\n```\ndata.terraform_remote_state.network:\n  id = 2016-10-29 01:57:59.780010914 +0000 UTC\n  addresses.# = 2\n  addresses.0 = 52.207.220.222\n  addresses.1 = 54.196.78.166\n  backend = s3\n  config.% = 3\n  config.bucket = terraform-state-prod\n  config.key = network/terraform.tfstate\n  config.region = us-east-1\n  elb_address = web-elb-790251200.us-east-1.elb.amazonaws.com\n  public_subnet_id = subnet-1e05dd33\n```\n\n## Configuration\n\nThis backend requires the configuration of the AWS Region and S3 state storage. Other configuration, such as enabling DynamoDB state locking, is optional.\n\n### Credentials and Shared Configuration\n\n!> **Warning:**  We recommend using environment variables to supply credentials and other sensitive data. If you use `-backend-config` or hardcode these values directly in your configuration, Terraform will include these values in both the `.terraform` subdirectory and in plan files. This workspace will not be used, but is created automatically\nby Terraform as a convenience for users who are not using the workspaces\nfeature.\n\nCreate a workspace corresponding to each key given in the `workspace_iam_roles`\nvariable value above:\n\n```\n$ terraform workspace new staging\nCreated and switched to workspace \"staging\"!\n\n...\n\n$ terraform workspace new production\nCreated and switched to workspace \"production\"!\n\n...\n```\n\nDue to the `assume_role` setting in the AWS provider configuration, any\nmanagement operations for AWS resources will be performed via the configured\nrole in the appropriate environment AWS account. The backend operations, such\nas reading and writing the state from S3, will be performed directly as the\nadministrator's own user within the administrative account.\n\n```\n$ terraform workspace select staging\n$ terraform apply\n...\n```\n\n### Running Terraform in Amazon EC2\n\nTeams that make extensive use of Terraform for infrastructure management\noften [run Terraform in automation](/terraform/tutorials/automation/automate-terraform?utm_source=WEBSITE&utm_medium=WEB_IO&utm_offer=ARTICLE_PAGE&utm_content=DOCS)\nto ensure a consistent operating environment and to limit access to the\nvarious secrets and other sensitive information that Terraform configurations\ntend to require.\n\nWhen running Terraform in an automation tool running on an Amazon EC2 instance,\nconsider running this instance in the administrative account and using an\n[instance profile](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_roles_use_switch-role-ec2_instance-profiles.html)\nin place of the various administrator IAM users suggested above. An IAM\ninstance profile can also be granted cross-account delegation access via\nan IAM policy, giving this instance the access it needs to run Terraform.\n\nTo isolate access to different environment accounts, use a separate EC2\ninstance for each target account so that its access can be limited only to\nthe single account.\n\nSimilar approaches can be taken with equivalent features in other AWS compute\nservices, such as ECS.\n\n### Protecting Access to Workspace State\n\nIn a simple implementation of the pattern described in the prior sections,\nall users have access to read and write states for all workspaces. In many\ncases it is desirable to apply more precise access constraints to the\nTerraform state objects in S3, so that for example only trusted administrators\nare allowed to modify the production state, or to control _reading_ of a state\nthat contains sensitive information.\n\nAmazon S3 supports fine-grained access control on a per-object-path basis\nusing IAM policy. A full description of S3's access control mechanism is\nbeyond the scope of this guide, but an example IAM policy granting access\nto only a single state object within an S3 bucket is shown below:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"Version\": \"2012-10-17\",\n  \"Statement\": [\n    {\n      \"Effect\": \"Allow\",\n      \"Action\": \"s3:ListBucket\",\n      \"Resource\": \"arn:aws:s3:::example-bucket\",\n      \"Condition\": {\n        \"StringEquals\": {\n          \"s3:prefix\": \"path/to/state\"\n        }\n      }\n    },\n    {\n      \"Effect\": \"Allow\",\n      \"Action\": [\"s3:GetObject\", \"s3:PutObject\"],\n      \"Resource\": \"arn:aws:s3:::example-bucket/myapp/production/tfstate\"\n    }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\nThe example backend configuration below documents the corresponding `bucket` and `key` arguments:\n\n```hcl\nterraform {\n  backend \"s3\" {\n    bucket = \"example-bucket\"\n    key    = \"path/to/state\"\n    region = \"us-east-1\"\n  }\n}\n```\n\nRefer to the [AWS documentation on S3 access control](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/s3-access-control.html) for more details.\n\nDynamoDB does not assign a separate resource ARN to each key in a table, but you can write more precise policies for a DynamoDB table [using an IAM `Condition` element](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/specifying-conditions.html).\nFor example, you can use the `dynamodb:LeadingKeys` condition key to match on the partition key values that the S3 backend will use:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"Version\": \"2012-10-17\",\n  \"Statement\": [\n    {\n      \"Effect\": \"Allow\",\n      \"Action\": [\n        \"dynamodb:DeleteItem\",\n        \"dynamodb:GetItem\",\n        \"dynamodb:PutItem\"\n      ],\n      \"Resource\": \"arn:aws:dynamodb:us-east-1:12341234:table/example-table\",\n      \"Condition\": {\n        \"ForAllValues:StringEquals\": {\n          \"dynamodb:LeadingKeys\": [\n            \"example-bucket/path/to/state\",\n            \"example-bucket/path/to/state-md5\"\n          ]\n        }\n      }\n    }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\nNote that DynamoDB ARNs are regional and account-specific, unlike S3 bucket ARNs, so you must also specify the correct region and AWS account ID for your DynamoDB table in the `Resource` element.\n\nThe example backend configuration below documents the corresponding arguments:\n\n```hcl\nterraform {\n  backend \"s3\" {\n    bucket         = \"example-bucket\"\n    key            = \"path/to/state\"\n    region         = \"us-east-1\"\n    dynamodb_table = \"example-table\"\n  }\n}\n```\n\nRefer to the [AWS documentation on DynamoDB fine-grained locking](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazondynamodb/latest/developerguide/specifying-conditions.html) for more details.\n\n### Configuring Custom User-Agent Information\n\nNote this feature is {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_019Vs2rfGSaYGMgTJqdrBEui","type":"tool_result","content":"---\npage_title: Conditional Expressions - Configuration Language\ndescription: >-\n  Conditional expressions select one of two values. You can use them to define\n  defaults to replace invalid values.\n---\n\n# Conditional Expressions\n\nA _conditional expression_ uses the value of a boolean expression to select one of\ntwo values.\n\n> **Hands-on:** Try the [Create Dynamic Expressions](/terraform/tutorials/configuration-language/expressions?utm_source=WEBSITE&utm_medium=WEB_IO&utm_offer=ARTICLE_PAGE&utm_content=DOCS) tutorial.\n\n## Syntax\n\nThe syntax of a conditional expression is as follows:\n\n```hcl\ncondition ? true_val : false_val\n```\n\nIf `condition` is `true` then the result is `true_val`. If `condition` is\n`false` then the result is `false_val`.\n\nA common use of conditional expressions is to define defaults to replace\ninvalid values:\n\n```hcl\nvar.a != \"\" ? var.a : \"default-a\"\n```\n\nIf `var.a` is an empty string then the result is `\"default-a\"`, but otherwise\nit is the actual value of `var.a`.\n\n## Conditions\n\nThe condition can be any expression that resolves to a boolean value. This will\nusually be an expression that uses the equality, comparison, or logical\noperators.\n\n### Custom Condition Checks\n\nYou can create conditions that produce custom error messages for several types of objects in a configuration. For example, you can add a condition to an input variable that checks whether incoming image IDs are formatted properly.\n\nCustom conditions can help capture assumptions, helping future maintainers understand the configuration design and intent. They also return useful information about errors earlier and in context, helping consumers more easily diagnose issues in their configurations.\n\nRefer to [Custom Condition Checks](/terraform/language/expressions/custom-conditions#input-variable-validation) for details.\n\n## Result Types\n\nThe two result values may be of any type, but they must both\nbe of the _same_ type so that Terraform can determine what type the whole\nconditional expression will return without knowing the condition value.\n\nIf the two result expressions don't produce the same type then Terraform will\nattempt to find a type that they can both convert to, and make those\nconversions automatically if so.\n\nFor example, the following expression is valid and will always return a string,\nbecause in Terraform all numbers can convert automatically to a string using\ndecimal digits:\n\n```hcl\nvar.example ? 12 : \"hello\"\n```\n\nRelying on this automatic conversion behavior can be confusing for those who\nare not familiar with Terraform's conversion rules though, so we recommend\nbeing explicit using type conversion functions in any situation where there may\nbe some uncertainty about the expected result type.\n\nThe following example is contrived because it would be easier to write the\nconstant `\"12\"` instead of the type conversion in this case, but shows how to\nuse [`tostring`](/terraform/language/functions/tostring) to explicitly convert a number to\na string.\n\n```hcl\nvar.example ? tostring(12) : \"hello\"\n```","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"ceac232f-983d-4180-b05d-2dff83d40a7a","uuid":"b265abb7-73a1-4c30-a709-ff4a815de655","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"---\npage_title: Conditional Expressions - Configuration Language\ndescription: >-\n  Conditional expressions select one of two values. If `condition` is\n`false` then the result is `false_val`.\n\nA common use of conditional expressions is to define defaults to replace\ninvalid values:\n\n```hcl\nvar.a != \"\" ? var.a : \"default-a\"\n```\n\nIf `var.a` is an empty string then the result is `\"default-a\"`, but otherwise\nit is the actual value of `var.a`.\n\n## Conditions\n\nThe condition can be any expression that resolves to a boolean value. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_017zdMriUgkd5LHxv5seztVQ","type":"tool_result","content":"---\npage_title: nonsensitive - Functions - Configuration Language\ndescription: >-\n  The nonsensitive function removes the sensitive marking from a value that\n  Terraform considers to be sensitive.\n---\n\n# `nonsensitive` Function\n\n-> **Note:** This function is only available in Terraform v0.15 and later.\n\n`nonsensitive` takes a sensitive value and returns a copy of that value with\nthe sensitive marking removed, thereby exposing the sensitive value.\n\n~> **Warning:** Using this function indiscriminately will cause values that\nTerraform would normally have considered as sensitive to be treated as normal\nvalues and shown clearly in Terraform's output. Use this function only when\nyou've derived a new value from a sensitive value in a way that eliminates the\nsensitive portions of the value.\n\nNormally Terraform tracks when you use expressions to derive a new value from\na value that is marked as sensitive, so that the result can also be marked\nas sensitive.\n\nHowever, you may wish to write expressions that derive non-sensitive results\nfrom sensitive values. For example, if you know based on details of your\nparticular system and its threat model that a SHA256 hash of a particular\nsensitive value is safe to include clearly in Terraform output, you could use\nthe `nonsensitive` function to indicate that, overriding Terraform's normal\nconservative behavior:\n\n```hcl\noutput \"sensitive_example_hash\" {\n  value = nonsensitive(sha256(var.sensitive_example))\n}\n```\n\nAnother example might be if the original value is only partially sensitive and\nyou've written expressions to separate the sensitive and non-sensitive parts:\n\n```hcl\nvariable \"mixed_content_json\" {\n  description = \"A JSON string containing a mixture of sensitive and non-sensitive values.\"\n  type        = string\n  sensitive   = true\n}\n\nlocals {\n  # mixed_content is derived from var.mixed_content_json, so it\n  # is also considered to be sensitive.\n  mixed_content = jsondecode(var.mixed_content_json)\n\n  # password_from_json is derived from mixed_content, so it's\n  # also considered to be sensitive.\n  password_from_json = local.mixed_content[\"password\"]\n\n  # username_from_json would normally be considered to be\n  # sensitive too, but system-specific knowledge tells us\n  # that the username is a non-sensitive fragment of the\n  # original document, and so we can override Terraform's\n  # determination.\n  username_from_json = nonsensitive(local.mixed_content[\"username\"])\n}\n```\n\nWhen you use this function, it's your responsibility to ensure that the\nexpression passed as its argument will remove all sensitive content from\nthe sensitive value it depends on. By passing a value to `nonsensitive` you are\ndeclaring to Terraform that you have done all that is necessary to ensure that\nthe resulting value has no sensitive content, even though it was derived\nfrom sensitive content. If a sensitive value appears in Terraform's output\ndue to an inappropriate call to `nonsensitive` in your module, that's a bug in\nyour module and not a bug in Terraform itself.\n**Use this function sparingly and only with due care.**\n\n`nonsensitive` will make no changes to values that aren't marked as sensitive, even though such a call may be redundant and potentially confusing.\nUse `nonsensitive` only after careful consideration and with definite intent.\n\nConsider including a comment adjacent to your call to explain to future\nmaintainers what makes the usage safe and thus what invariants they must take\ncare to preserve under future modifications.\n\n## Examples\n\nThe following examples are from `terraform console` when running in the\ncontext of the example above with `variable \"mixed_content_json\"` and\nthe local value `mixed_content`, with a valid JSON string assigned to\n`var.mixed_content_json`.\n\n```\n> var.mixed_content_json\n(sensitive value)\n> local.mixed_content\n(sensitive value)\n> local.mixed_content[\"password\"]\n(sensitive value)\n> nonsensitive(local.mixed_content[\"username\"])\n\"zqb\"\n> nonsensitive(\"clear\")\n\nError: Invalid function argument\n\nInvalid value for \"value\" parameter: the given value is not sensitive, so this\ncall is redundant.\n```\n\nNote though that it's always your responsibility to use `nonsensitive` only\nwhen it's safe to do so. If you use `nonsensitive` with content that\n_ought to be_ considered sensitive then that content will be disclosed:\n\n```\n> nonsensitive(var.mixed_content_json)\n<<EOT\n{\n  \"username\": \"zqb\",\n  \"password\": \"p4ssw0rd\"\n}\nEOT\n> nonsensitive(local.mixed_content)\n{\n  \"password\" = \"p4ssw0rd\"\n  \"username\" = \"zqb\"\n}\n> nonsensitive(local.mixed_content[\"password\"])\n\"p4ssw0rd\"\n```\n---\npage_title: issensitive - Functions - Configuration Language\ndescription: The issensitive function true if the value passed is marked as sensitive\n---\n\n# `issensitive` Function\n\n-> **Note:** This function is only available in Terraform v1.8 and later.\n\n`issensitive` takes any value and returns true if Terraform\ntreats it as sensitive, with the same meaning and behavior as for\n[sensitive input variables](/terraform/language/values/variables#suppressing-values-in-cli-output).\n\nIf a value not marked as sensitive is passed the function returns false.\n\nSee [`sensitive`](/terraform/language/functions/sensitive), [`nonsensitive`](/terraform/language/functions/nonsensitive), and [sensitive input variables](/terraform/language/values/variables#suppressing-values-in-cli-output) for more information on sensitive values.\n\n## Examples\n\n```\n> issensitive(sensitive(\"secret\"))\ntrue\n> issensitive(\"hello\")\nfalse\n> sensitive(var.my-var-with-sensitive-set-to-true)\ntrue\n```","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"ceac232f-983d-4180-b05d-2dff83d40a7a","uuid":"1bb2d076-6cb2-4afb-88ee-effd193c06e0","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"---\npage_title: nonsensitive -
